# ROUND 2/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 per moving robot → robots should be STATIONARY (Stop)
- After switch time: score += 1 per moving robot... wait, re-reading: "score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving" after switch. So after switch, moving robots INCREASE score. We want robots to be stationary both before AND after switch? No wait...

Re-reading: "Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is **stationary**." So before switch, stationary robots are BAD. After switch, moving robots are BAD.

So:
- **Before switch**: robots should MOVE (to minimize score before switch)
- **After switch**: robots should STOP (to minimize score after switch)

The signal for the switch is the GREEN wall lighting up. Robots can detect this with C=7 (perceive green color, color index 2).

**Optimal strategy**: Start moving, then switch to Stop when green is detected.

**State design**:
- State 0: Exploration (moving) → transition to Stop when green detected (C=7, color=2)
- State 1: Stop → stay stopped

The champion (always Stop) scores ~9929 because it's stationary before switch (~500 steps × 20 robots = ~10000 penalty before switch, ~0 after). Moving before switch would save those ~10000 points but cost ~700 steps × 20 robots after switch if they don't stop.

Wait: champion score is 9929. If all robots stop always: penalty = steps_before_switch × 20. With switch at ~500 steps average, that's ~10000. After switch with all stopped = 0. Total ≈ 10000. That matches!

If we move before and stop after green: penalty before = 0, penalty after = depends on how quickly they detect/respond to green. This should be much better.

**Key insight**: 2-state FSM: Move → detect green → Stop should score near 0 (just the detection lag).

Let me design the 14 machines:

1. **Survive champion**: 1 state, always Stop
2-8. **2-state: Explore then Stop on green** (main strategy, varied params)
9-11. **3-state variants** with more robust transitions
12-14. **Exploration variants**

FSM color for green LED: let's use 2 (green) to help neighbors see the signal? Actually LEDs don't matter for scoring.

For C=7 detecting green: color index 2 = green.

## Machines

**FSM 1 (champion survival):** Always stop

**FSM 2-8:** 2-state: State0=Exploration, transition to Stop on green detection

**FSM 9-11:** 3-state variants

**FSM 12-14:** Additional exploration

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion uses GoToColor(green) → Stop when green detected. Score 7648.57.

**Why is this not near 0?**
- GoToColor(green) moves toward the green wall, which means robots ARE moving before the switch (good, reduces pre-switch penalty)
- After switch, green appears → transition to Stop (good)
- But the problem: robots start in GoToColor(green) state, but there's NO green initially. So GoToColor without a visible target... what does it do? It probably wanders or stops. If it stops/slows when no target, that's bad before switch.

Actually, the key insight: before switch, no green wall exists, so GoToColor(green) has no target to go to. It might behave like exploration or stop. The transition `c0x0 7 --l0x0 2` fires when green is perceived - so once green appears, robots switch to Stop.

Score of 7648 means roughly 7648/20 = 382 steps equivalent of penalty. If switch happens at ~500 steps and robots are stationary before (bad) for 500 steps: 500×20 = 10000. With score 7648, we're saving ~2352 penalty units. So GoToColor(green) with no target is moving somewhat.

**Better approach**: Use plain Exploration before switch (definitely moving), then detect green and Stop.

The best 2-state FSM should be:
- State 0: Exploration (moves always) → detect green → Stop
- State 1: Stop → stays

The champion's GoToColor approach scored 7648. My Exploration→Stop machines should have scored better but apparently the champion came from my FSM 6 (GoToColor). Let me check what happened to the pure Exploration→Stop machines.

The issue might be: when robots are exploring and green appears, they detect it and stop. But maybe the `--p0x0 1.0` probability helps vs lower values.

**Key improvements to try:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploration with high rwm → Stop on green (p=1.0)
3. Multiple transitions: detect green OR many neighbors (cascade signaling)
4. GoToColor(green) with better stop condition
5. Attraction-based spreading before switch, then Stop on green

The champion uses `--clr0 2` which is green. Let me think about whether GoToColor(green) before green exists causes the robot to move or stop. If no green object visible, it may just spin/wander. This seems to work better than pure exploration perhaps due to less energy wasted.

Let me focus on variants that clearly move before switch and reliably stop after:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion is GoToColor(green) → Stop on green detected, with stop state having p=0.01 probability of going back. Score 7165.37.

**Why is this still ~7165?**

Let me think more carefully. The switch time is between 400-600 steps. The run is 1200 steps. 

- Before switch (~500 steps avg): robots should MOVE. GoToColor(green) with no green visible - what does this behavior do? It likely wanders/explores since there's no target. Score before switch ≈ 0 (robots moving).
- After switch (~700 steps remaining): green appears. Robots detect green and Stop. Score after switch ≈ 0 once stopped.

But score is 7165. That's 7165/20 = 358 equivalent robot-steps of penalty. 

Wait - the `--p1x0 0.01` means the Stop state has a 1% chance per step of transitioning back to GoToColor. That means after stopping, robots occasionally re-enter the moving state. Over 700 steps, expected returns = 700 × 0.01 = 7 times, each lasting some steps. This costs penalty!

**Key fix**: Make the Stop state truly permanent (probability 0 or very small of leaving). The transition back from Stop is hurting us.

But also: before switch with GoToColor(green) and no target, do robots actually move? If the behavior makes robots stand still when no green is visible, then the pre-switch penalty is large (like the always-Stop baseline at ~10000).

Champion score 7165 < 9929 (always stop), so GoToColor must be moving somewhat before switch.

**Main improvements:**
1. Reduce or eliminate the back-transition from Stop (p1x0 very small or 0... but min is 0.001?)
2. Actually `--p1x0 0.005` was tried in round 2 and scored 7648, while `--p1x0 0.01` scored 7165. Hmm, higher p means MORE returns to moving, which should be worse after switch... unless it's helping before switch somehow?

Wait - maybe before switch GoToColor(green) isn't moving much, so the p1x0 helps robots re-enter GoToColor and move more. If robots are stationary in GoToColor before switch (no target = no movement), then a higher p1x0 would cause them to re-enter GoToColor from Stop more often... but they start in GoToColor, not Stop.

Actually the real issue: GoToColor(green) with no green visible probably just goes straight or wanders. It may or may not count as "moving" (threshold is 0.0005m per step). It likely does move.

**Better idea**: Use Exploration (definitely moves) before switch, with a PERMANENT stop after green detected. The issue in round 2 was my Exploration machines scored worse. Why? Maybe detection lag - not all robots can see the green wall immediately.

**Cascade signaling**: robots that see green stop and emit a signal (LED color), nearby robots detect the signal and also stop. But robots can't directly perceive neighbor LEDs via the conditions provided (only floor color, neighbor count, green wall).

Actually, we could use neighbor count: once some robots stop, the stopped robots form clusters, and neighbor-count transitions could propagate the stop. But this is complex.

**Simplest strong improvement**: Try Exploration → Stop with truly zero back-transition (p=0.001, the minimum).

Also try: what if state 1 (Stop) has NO outgoing transition? But rules say every state must have `--n<I>` ≥ 1. So minimum is 1 transition with very low probability.

Let me try p1x0 = 0.001 (minimum) and p1x0 = 0.0 isn't allowed. Also try varying rwm for exploration.

Also: the champion's `--p0x0 1` means 100% probability of transitioning to Stop when green is seen. That's good.

**New topologies to explore:**
- 3-state: Explore → detect green → GoToColor(green) to approach wall → Stop (maybe confirming green?)
- 2-state with Repulsion (spreading out so more robots can see the wall)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion is GoToColor(green) → Stop on green detected, with p1x0=0.02 (2% chance per step of leaving Stop back to GoToColor). Score 6782.46.

**Interesting trend**: p=0.005 → 7648, p=0.01 → 7165, p=0.02 → 6782. Higher back-transition probability is IMPROVING the score. This seems counterintuitive - why would going back to GoToColor help?

**Hypothesis**: Before the switch, there's NO green wall. GoToColor(green) with no visible green... the behavior might actually cause the robot to stop or barely move (no target). If that's the case, robots in GoToColor state before switch are STATIONARY → penalty accumulates (like the always-Stop baseline). 

When p1x0 is higher, robots that accidentally entered Stop (via some other mechanism) get back to GoToColor quickly. But wait, the only transition OUT of state 0 is detecting green (C=7, l=2). Before switch there's no green, so robots stay in GoToColor the whole pre-switch period.

**Alternative hypothesis**: GoToColor(green) does move the robot (maybe it spins/wanders when no target), and after switch when green appears, robots transition to Stop. The p1x0 transition back to GoToColor after stopping is GOOD because once stopped, if a robot occasionally re-enters GoToColor(green), it moves toward the green wall - but the green wall is a wall, so GoToColor makes them hit the wall and stop there (physically stopped by wall). Then the C=7 condition fires again and returns to Stop state. So the cycle: Stop → (0.02 prob) → GoToColor(green) → (immediately sees green) → Stop. During that brief GoToColor moment, the robot moves toward wall = counts as MOVING = penalty after switch!

Wait no - after switch, moving costs penalty. So returning to GoToColor after switch is BAD.

**Third hypothesis**: GoToColor(green) before switch = robot IS moving (exploring/spinning looking for green). After switch, green appears, robot stops. The p1x0=0.02 means some robots re-enter GoToColor after switch, move briefly, detect green again, stop. This costs a tiny bit after switch but the cycle is short.

The improvement from 0.005→0.01→0.02 might be: with higher p1x0, robots are more "refreshed" in their GoToColor behavior - maybe GoToColor has some internal state that gets reset, causing more movement. Or perhaps I'm wrong about the direction.

**Let me try higher values**: p1x0 = 0.03, 0.05, 0.1 to see if trend continues.

Also, the fundamental question: does GoToColor(green) move robots when no green is visible? If yes, it's good before switch. If no, we need Exploration before switch.

Given the trend, let me push p1x0 higher and also try Exploration with high back-transition.

Also consider: what if Stop state transitions back with C=7 (green) at high probability? That would mean: once stopped, if still seeing green, re-enter GoToColor. But that's bad after switch.

**Better idea for Stop state**: transition with C=4 (neighbors BELOW threshold) - if isolated (no neighbors), re-enter moving state. This could help robots spread out.

Let me also try GoAwayColor(green) before switch → Stop after switch. GoAway might move more reliably.

Key explorations:
1. Increase p1x0 beyond 0.02 (0.03, 0.05, 0.1)
2. Try Exploration with various p1x0 values  
3. Try GoAwayColor(green) → Stop
4. Try 3-state with spreading + stop
5. Multiple transitions from Stop

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The trend is clear: higher p1x0 → better score.
- p=0.005: 7648
- p=0.01: 7165
- p=0.02: 6782
- p=0.05: 6277

This strongly suggests that GoToColor(green) IS moving robots (even before switch), and the key insight is: **the Stop state is actually BAD before the switch** because before switch, stationary robots incur penalty. With higher p1x0, robots spend less time in Stop and more time in GoToColor(moving).

Wait - but before switch, there's no green wall. So robots start in GoToColor(green), no green is visible, so the C=7 transition never fires. Robots stay in GoToColor the entire pre-switch period. They should all be moving → good.

After switch, green appears. Some robots see it and transition to Stop. Then with p1x0=0.05, they have 5% chance per step of going back to GoToColor. After switch, moving is BAD. So higher p1x0 should hurt after switch...

**Unless** I'm wrong about what phase penalizes what. Let me re-read:
- "Before switch: score += 1 per stationary robot"
- "After switch: score += 1 per moving robot"

So:
- Before switch: MOVING is good (no penalty), STATIONARY is bad
- After switch: STATIONARY is good (no penalty), MOVING is bad

With GoToColor(green):
- Before switch: no green → robots in GoToColor → they move → no penalty ✓
- After switch: green appears → C=7 fires → robots go to Stop → no penalty ✓
- But with p1x0, some robots randomly leave Stop → move → penalty ✗

So higher p1x0 should be WORSE after switch. But the data shows it's BETTER. 

**New hypothesis**: Maybe GoToColor(green) WITHOUT a visible target causes robots to STOP (stand still), not move. If that's the case:
- Before switch: no green → GoToColor → robots STATIONARY → large penalty
- After switch: green appears → C=7 fires → Stop → no change (already stationary) → no penalty
- With p1x0: after stopping (redundantly), 5% chance to re-enter GoToColor → still stationary (no green? wait, green IS visible after switch) → green visible → immediately transitions back to Stop

Hmm, but after switch green IS visible. So GoToColor(green) would move toward it. The cycle after switch: Stop → (0.05 prob) → GoToColor(green) → sees green immediately → Stop. During the brief GoToColor moment, the robot moves → penalty.

**But then why is higher p1x0 better?**

Maybe the real answer: GoToColor with NO target makes robots spin in place. Spinning counts as moving (>0.0005m traveled). So before switch: GoToColor = moving = no penalty. After switch: Stop is good. The cycling back to GoToColor after switch causes brief movement = small penalty. But this is outweighed by... what?

Actually wait - maybe the issue is that after switch, not ALL robots immediately see the green wall. Only robots facing/near the green wall can perceive it. Robots that can't see green remain in GoToColor state. GoToColor robots that CAN'T see green continue moving → penalty after switch.

With higher p1x0: robots that DID see green and stopped have a 5% chance to re-enter GoToColor. If they then immediately see green again, they stop instantly. This creates "refreshed" attention to green. Maybe this helps robots that were in a bad orientation to re-orient and find/stop.

OR: the p1x0 back-transition is helping robots that enter Stop state BEFORE the switch (if C=7 fires spuriously somehow). They'd be stationary before switch → penalty. The p1x0 gets them back to moving. But C=7 only fires on green, and green doesn't appear before switch...

**Most likely**: GoToColor with no target = robot moves forward/wanders. After switch, green appears, robots that see it stop. Robots that DON'T see it stay in GoToColor moving → this is the main cost. Higher p1x0 doesn't help this.

The trend 0.005→0.05 improving might just be statistical noise in the optimizer. Let me continue the trend to higher values (0.1, 0.15, 0.2) and also try genuinely different approaches.

**Alternative**: What if p1x0 is the probability of transitioning, and higher p means robots re-enter GoToColor more often BEFORE switch when they somehow ended up in Stop? No, they can't end up in Stop before switch without seeing green.

I'll push p1x0 higher (0.1, 0.15, 0.2, 0.3) and also try fundamentally different approaches: pure Exploration with similar back-transitions, and 3-state machines with cascade signaling.

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --cle0 0 --clr0 2 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 1 --l0x0 2 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.05

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion hasn't improved from round 5 to round 6 (still 6277.95). The trend of higher p1x0 improving score seems to have plateaued or reversed. Let me reconsider fundamentally.

**Deep analysis of why GoToColor(green)+Stop works at all:**

The score of 6277 out of a maximum of 1200×20=24000 means robots are incurring ~6278 penalty steps. 

If switch is at step 500 (average):
- Before switch (500 steps): if robots are moving → 0 penalty. If stationary → 500×20=10000 penalty.
- After switch (700 steps): if robots are stopped → 0 penalty. If moving → 700×20=14000 penalty.

Score 6277 suggests ~314 steps worth of 20-robot penalty, or some mix. GoToColor(green) with no target must be causing some stationary time (maybe ~300 steps stationary before switch = 6000 penalty). After switch robots quickly stop.

**Key question**: Why did p1x0=0.05 score 6277 but p1x0=0.1, 0.15, 0.2, 0.3 didn't improve? The optimizer likely evaluated many variants in round 6 and none beat 6277. This suggests we're near a local optimum with this topology.

**Fundamental rethink**: The issue is that GoToColor(green) with NO visible green target might make robots move randomly or stop. If they're stopping, we need them to move before switch.

What if we use a DIFFERENT color for the pre-switch moving behavior? GoToColor on a color that doesn't exist in the arena will cause... what? Same issue.

**What if the floor is GRAY?** C=1 (gray floor) fires with some probability. All robots are on gray floor always. So `--c<I>x<K> 1 --p<I>x<K> X` fires with probability X per step when on gray (always).

**New idea**: What if Stop state uses C=1 (always on gray) with high probability to return to GoToColor? This is essentially equivalent to C=5 with the same probability since floor is always gray. But maybe there's a subtle difference.

**Better new idea**: The problem might be that not all robots can see the green wall (it's one wall, some robots face away). Robots that can't see green stay in GoToColor and keep moving after switch → penalty. 

**Cascade signaling**: Use robot LEDs to propagate the "stop" signal. If robots that see green emit a colored LED, and nearby robots detect that LED color... but C=7 only detects object colors (walls/lights), not robot LEDs directly. Actually, do robot LEDs appear as colored objects to other robots' cameras? If yes, we could use this!

If robots in Stop state emit LED color 4 (yellow), and other robots have transition C=7 with color 4, then stopped robots propagate the stop signal. Let me try this.

**3-state cascade**:
- State 0: GoToColor(green), LED=off → detect green → State 1
- State 1: Stop, LED=yellow → detect yellow (neighbor stopped) OR random → State 1 stays... 

Wait, transitions must go to OTHER states. So:
- State 0: GoToColor(green), LED=0 → detect green (C=7,l=2) → State 1
- State 1: Stop, LED=4(yellow) → small prob (C=5) → State 0 (cycle back)
- OR: State 0 → detect yellow LED from stopped neighbor → State 1

Let me try:
- State 0: GoToColor(green), LED=0, transitions: detect green→Stop, detect yellow (neighbor stopped)→Stop
- State 1: Stop, LED=4(yellow), transition: small prob→State 0

This way, robots that can't see the green wall but ARE near a robot that stopped (and emits yellow) will also stop.

Also try: Exploration before switch with cascade.

Let me also try: what if GoToColor(green) = moving before switch is wrong, and the real solution is Exploration? Let me try Exploration with various rwm values and p1x0=0.05.

And: what about using neighbor count? After switch, many robots stop. A stopped robot has many stationary neighbors. A robot could transition to Stop when it detects BELOW threshold neighbors (isolated) or ABOVE threshold (crowded = stopped robots nearby)?

Actually let me try the cascade LED approach and also vary the champion parameters more carefully.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Massive improvement! Score jumped from 6277 to 1998. The cascade signaling worked!

**Champion analysis:**
- State 0: GoToColor(green), LED=off. TWO transitions: 
  - detect green (C=7, l=2, p=1.0) → Stop
  - detect yellow LED (C=7, l=4, p=1.0) → Stop (cascade: neighbor stopped = also stop)
- State 1: Stop, LED=yellow(4). One transition: random (C=5, p=0.05) → GoToColor

**Why it works so well:**
- Before switch: GoToColor(green) with no green visible → robots move (0 pre-switch penalty)
- After switch: green wall appears → some robots see it and stop, emit yellow LED
- Nearby robots detect yellow LEDs from stopped neighbors → also stop (cascade propagation!)
- This propagates the stop signal even to robots that can't see the green wall directly

**Score of 1998**: 1998/20 = ~100 step-equivalents of penalty. This is excellent! Most penalty probably comes from:
1. Detection lag after switch (robots that haven't yet received the cascade signal)
2. The p=0.05 back-transition occasionally sending stopped robots back to moving

**How to improve further:**

1. **Reduce back-transition probability**: p1x0=0.05 sends robots back to GoToColor. After switch, they see green immediately and stop again, but that brief move costs penalty. Try p=0.02, 0.01.

2. **Faster cascade**: Add more cascade triggers. Currently detects yellow (l=4). What if GoToColor state also detects other colors? Or use a 3-state approach with GoToColor(green) → GoToColor(yellow) → Stop?

3. **Add cascade to Stop state too**: If in Stop, see yellow neighbors → stay (but can't transition to self). Actually Stop should stay stopped. The p=0.05 back-transition is a problem.

4. **Try p0x0 < 1.0 for green detection** to see if there's benefit, but likely not.

5. **Multiple cascade colors**: What if robots in stop state emit different colors and we detect multiple colors?

6. **3-state with better cascade**: 
   - State 0: GoToColor(green), detect green OR yellow → State 1
   - State 1: Stop, LED=yellow, p=0.001 → State 0 (very small back-transition)
   - This keeps the cascade but reduces the back-prob

7. **What about GoToColor(yellow)?** After switch, stopped robots emit yellow. Moving robots go toward yellow (toward stopped robots). Being near stopped robots means they're near the "stopped zone" and more likely to detect yellow → stop.

8. **Try different LED colors for cascade** (cyan=6, magenta=5) to avoid any false positives.

The key improvement is reducing p1x0 while keeping the cascade. Also try adding GoToColor(yellow) as intermediate state.

Let me also think: with p=0.05, after switch a robot stops, then with p=0.05 per step returns to GoToColor for ~1 step (sees green immediately, stops). Each cycle costs ~1 step of moving. Over 700 post-switch steps with 20 robots: 700×0.05×1 step×20 = 700 cycles. But score is only 1998, so it's not that bad.

Actually the 1998 score might mostly be from robots that can't see the green wall AND can't see any yellow-LED robots (isolated robots after switch). They stay in GoToColor moving indefinitely.

**Best fix**: Make cascade more robust. Add neighbor count transitions: if many neighbors (cluster of stopped robots nearby) → also stop.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Incredible improvement! Score dropped from 1998 to 250! The key was reducing p1x0 from 0.05 to 0.001.

**Champion analysis:**
- State 0: GoToColor(green), LED=off. Transitions: detect green (p=1.0) → Stop, detect yellow LED (p=1.0) → Stop
- State 1: Stop, LED=yellow(4). Transition: random (p=0.001) → GoToColor

**Why 250 is the remaining score:**
With p=0.001, robots in Stop leave only rarely (1/1000 per step). Over 700 post-switch steps: 700×0.001×20 = 14 transitions back to GoToColor. Each costs ~1 step of movement. That's ~14 penalty steps total. But score is 250/20 = 12.5 equivalent robot-steps... wait, 250 total with 20 robots = 12.5 steps average. That matches!

So the remaining score is almost entirely from:
1. The brief p=0.001 back-transitions (14 robot-steps ≈ 14 penalty)
2. Detection lag after switch (some robots don't immediately see green or yellow) - maybe a few robot-steps
3. Statistical variance

**Can we do better?**
- Try p1x0=0.0005 or 0.0001 - but minimum is 0.001 per the rules? Actually the rule says "real in [0..1]" for C=5. Let me check: `--p<I>x<K> real in [0..1]`. So 0.0005 might be valid!
- Try p1x0=0.0005, 0.0002, 0.0001
- Try adding more cascade triggers to state 0 to speed up convergence after switch
- Try 3-state with even better cascade

**Key remaining issue**: After switch, robots that are isolated (can't see green wall, can't see yellow-LED robots) stay in GoToColor and keep moving → penalty. Adding neighbor count transition could help: if robot is isolated (few neighbors), it might explore toward the green wall. If it has neighbors (stopped robots nearby), stop.

Actually wait - if robots are clustered near the green wall (they GoToColor = move toward green), they'll be close together, making cascade fast. The isolation issue might be minor.

**Try:**
1. p1x0 = 0.0005, 0.0002, 0.0001 (even smaller back-prob)
2. Add neighbor-count transition to state 0 (if many neighbors = stopped cluster nearby → stop)
3. Add additional cascade detection (detect other colors)
4. 3-state: GoToColor(green) → detect green/yellow → GoToColor(yellow, approach stopped cluster) → Stop
5. Try GoToColor(yellow) as state 0 initially... but before switch no yellow exists, so it wanders

Actually the minimum p for C=5 - the rules say "real in [0..1]" which includes very small values. Let me try 0.0001.

Also: the back-transition of p=0.001 causes robots to briefly re-enter GoToColor after switch, see green immediately (since green wall is visible), and stop again. This costs 1 step. At p=0.001, over 700 steps per robot: ~0.7 expected returns. With 20 robots: ~14 penalty steps. Score ≈ 14 + detection lag.

If we set p=0.0001: ~1.4 penalty steps from back-transitions. Detection lag might dominate.

**Can we improve detection speed?** Add transition from state 0: detect yellow LED (already there) AND detect neighbor count above threshold (many nearby robots = stopped cluster → stop too). But stopped robots don't move, so neighbor count among moving robots...

Actually, stopped robots ARE neighbors if within 0.5m. A moving robot approaching the stopped cluster will detect many neighbors → could trigger stop.

Let me add C=3 (neighbors above threshold) to state 0 as additional stop trigger.

Also: what if the Stop state emits a brighter/different signal? Already using yellow (4). Could try cyan (6) or magenta (5) - but the cascade is working with yellow.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion hasn't improved from round 8 to round 9 (still 250.78), despite trying p1x0 values of 0.0005, 0.0002, 0.0001 and adding neighbor-count transitions. This suggests 250.78 is near the optimum for this topology, and smaller p1x0 values either don't help or the optimizer couldn't find better.

**Why is 250.78 the floor?**

With 20 robots, 1200 steps, switch at ~500:
- Pre-switch (~500 steps): all robots moving (GoToColor with no green target) → 0 penalty ✓
- Post-switch (~700 steps): robots stop via cascade → near 0 penalty

Score 250.78 / 20 robots ≈ 12.5 robot-steps of penalty. This could come from:
1. Detection lag: after switch, the few steps before cascade propagates (~2-3 steps × a few robots)
2. The p=0.001 back-transitions: 700 steps × 0.001 × 20 = 14 expected back-transitions, each ~1 step = 14 penalty
3. Robots that are truly isolated and never receive the cascade

**What hasn't been tried:**
1. The neighbor-count transitions didn't help (round 9 didn't improve). Maybe they interfere with pre-switch behavior (robots cluster → neighbor count high → transition to Stop before switch → penalty!)
2. Different LED colors for cascade - tried yellow(4), cyan(6), magenta(5) - results not reported as champion, so they didn't beat 250.78
3. What about making Stop state emit LED AND having GoToColor state detect that LED at lower probability? Already at p=1.0.
4. **What if the Stop state has NO back-transition?** The rules require at least 1 transition (`--n<I>` in [1..4]). Minimum p for C=5 is... the rules say "real in [0..1]". Can we use 0.0? Let me try p=0.0 - though this might crash. Better to use a very tiny value.

Actually wait - can C=5 with p=0.0 work? The description says "fires with a fixed probability" - p=0.0 means never fires. This might be valid but could also crash. Let me try p=0.00001 as effectively zero.

5. **What if we use a DIFFERENT condition for the back-transition?** Instead of random C=5, use C=1 (gray floor, p very low) or C=4 (neighbors below threshold with very high steepness so it almost never fires).

6. **The real remaining penalty source**: Some robots after switch cannot see the green wall AND cannot see any yellow-LED robots because they're too far away (>0.5m range). These robots stay in GoToColor indefinitely → large penalty. 

**Solution for isolated robots**: Add GoToColor(yellow) as a secondary state. If a robot can't see green but starts moving toward yellow-LED robots (stopped cluster), it will eventually get close enough to detect yellow and stop.

**3-state cascade with attraction:**
- State 0: GoToColor(green), LED=off → detect green → State 1; detect yellow → State 1
- State 1: Stop, LED=yellow → p=0.001 → State 2
- State 2: GoToColor(yellow), LED=off → detect green → State 1; detect yellow → State 1

Actually this might oscillate. Better:
- State 0: GoToColor(green), LED=off → detect green OR yellow → State 1
- State 1: Stop, LED=yellow → p=0.001 → State 0

That's the champion. The issue is isolated robots.

**Try GoToColor(yellow) as the initial state:**
- Before switch: no yellow exists → robot wanders/moves → good
- After switch: some robots stop (how? they need to see green first...) 

**Better 3-state:**
- State 0: GoToColor(green), detect green→State1, detect yellow→State1
- State 1: Stop, LED=yellow, p=0.001→State2
- State 2: GoToColor(yellow), detect green→State1, detect yellow→State1

State 2 allows robots that briefly leave Stop to immediately re-find the yellow cluster. Also, robots in State 0 that can't see green but pass near the yellow cluster enter State 1.

**Try Attraction to neighbors as pre-switch behavior?** No - robots start clustered and attraction keeps them clustered → cascade works but pre-switch they might be stationary if no movement stimulus.

Let me focus on:
1. Champion (must include)
2. Variants with very small p1x0 (0.00001, 0.0001)
3. 3-state with GoToColor(yellow) as recovery state
4. Adding neighbor-count to STOP triggers (for isolated robots approaching cluster)
5. Try different back-transition conditions (C=1 gray with tiny prob)

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
